Appendix A
Norms

We define several useful norms: first the
trace norm
, which is defined over matrices, and then the
induced trace norm
and
diamond norm
, which are defined over linear superoperators (linear maps that act on matrices). We will also explain why, when it comes to evaluating how close two quantum channels are, the diamond norm is the most relevant notion (see Refs
[
43
,
44
]
for more details).

The trace norm.
The
trace norm

\|\cdot\|_{1}
is defined over matrices as follows:

\|X\|_{1}\coloneqq\operatorname{Tr}\sqrt{X^{{\dagger}}X}

If
X
is Hermitian,
\|X\|_{1}
is the sum of the absolute values of its eigenvalues:

\|X\|_{1}=\sum_{\lambda\in Sp(X)}|\lambda|

The trace norm is closely related to the
trace distance
, which is a standard way of evaluating the closeness of two quantum states. The trace distance between two quantum states
\sigma
and
\rho
is given by

D(\sigma,\rho)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\|\sigma-\rho\|_{1}.

We always have
0\leq D(\sigma,\rho)\leq 1
, with
D(\sigma,\rho)=0
if and only if
\sigma=\rho
and
D(\sigma,\rho)=1
if and only if
\sigma
and
\rho
have orthogonal support, i.e. they can be perfectly distinguished by some quantum measurement. In the case of pure states, the trace distance may be reformulated as follows:

D(\left|{\psi}\right\rangle\left\langle{\psi}\right|,\left|{\varphi}\right\rangle\left\langle{\varphi}\right|)=\sqrt{1-|\left\langle{\psi}\right|\varphi\rangle|^{2}}.

Notice that the distance is 1 if and only if the states are orthogonal and 0 if and only if
\left|{\psi}\right\rangle=e^{i\theta}\left|{\varphi}\right\rangle
for some
\theta
.

Lemma A.1

(Properties of the trace norm)
.

The following properties hold.

•

For all quantum state
\rho
,
\|\rho\|_{1}=1
,

•

\|X\otimes Y\|_{1}=\|X\|_{1}\|Y\|_{1}
.

The induced trace norm.
The
induced trace norm

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
is defined as the usual operator norm with respect to the trace norm
\|\cdot\|_{1}
. For any linear superoperator
\mathcal{C}
:

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\coloneqq\sup_{\rho\neq 0}\frac{\|\mathcal{C}(\rho)\|_{1}}{\|\rho\|_{1}}=\sup_{\|\rho\|_{1}=1}\|\mathcal{C}(\rho)\|_{1}.

Here
\rho
can be any non-zero matrix, not necssarily a quantum state.

This is not the norm we will use in order to evaluate the closeness of quantum channel. The first reason is that the induced trace norm is not stable under tensor product with the identity. Ref
[
43
]
provides the following counterexample: the transpose map
T:\left|{i}\right\rangle\left\langle{j}\right|\mapsto\left|{j}\right\rangle\left\langle{i}\right|
for
i,j=0,1
, acting on a single qubit system.
Moreover,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
isn’t multiplicative with respect to tensor products
[
44
]
. Therefore, we will use the diamond norm instead, for which both of these properties hold. The definition of the diamond norm is based on that of
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
:

The diamond norm.
The
diamond norm
(or
completely bounded trace norm
)
\|\cdot\|_{\diamond}
is defined as follows over the space of superoperators:

\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\diamond}\coloneqq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{I}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}

If
\mathcal{C}
acts on system
A
, then
E
is chosen to be isomorphic to
A
.

These two norms have the following properties. Importantly,
\|\cdot\|_{\diamond}
is muliplicative with respect to tensor products.

Lemma A.2

(Properties of the induced trace norm and diamond norm)
.

The following properties hold.

•

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{D}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
,

•

For all positive and trace preserving map
\mathcal{C}
,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathcal{C}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}=1
,

•

\|\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{C}\|_{\diamond}\leq\|\mathcal{D}\|_{\diamond}\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\diamond}
,

•

\|\mathcal{D}\otimes\mathcal{C}\|_{\diamond}=\|\mathcal{D}\|_{\diamond}\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\diamond}
,

•

For all quantum channel
\mathcal{C}
,
\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\diamond}=1
.

(See Refs.
[
43
,
44
]
for proofs of these statements.)

The diamond norm is also stable under tensor with the identity map:

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a linear superoperator and
E
and auxiliary system. We have
\|\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{E}\|_{\diamond}=\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\diamond}
.

Lastly, it is shown in Ref.
[
44
]
that the diamond norm can be expressed as follows in the case of Hermitian-preserving maps (with our notations):

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Hermitian-preserving linear map acting on system
A
. Then we have:

\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\diamond}=\sup_{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle\left\langle{\psi}\right|\in\mathsf{St}(EA)}\|(\mathcal{I}_{E}\otimes\mathcal{C})(\left|{\psi}\right\rangle\left\langle{\psi}\right|)\|_{1},

where
E
is isomorphic to
A
.

This matches the definition we gave in Section
2.1
, where we only considered the norm of Hermitian-preserving maps. (Indeed, we show in Lemma
2.1
that the supremum can equivalently be taken over pure states.) In particular, the difference of two quantum channels is Hermitian preserving, so we use this expression, which is easier to calculate.

A useful result on the distance between two quantum channels given in
terms of the diamond norm has been proved in
Ref.
[
39
]
:

Lemma A.4
.

Consider two channels

\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{1}\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathrm{A})
that can be
realized as random unitary transformations from the same set of
orthogonal unitaries, namely

\mathcal{E}_{n}(\cdot)=\sum_{i}p^{(n)}_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}(\cdot)
, with

\sum_{i}p_{i}^{(n)}=1
, for
n=1,2
. Then one has

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{E}_{1}-\mathcal{E}_{2}\|_{\diamond}=

\displaystyle\max_{\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\mathrm{E}\mathrm{A})}\|(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{E}}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{1})\rho-(\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{E}}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{2})\rho\|_{1}=2\sum_{i}|c_{i}|,

\displaystyle c_{i}=p_{i}^{(1)}-p_{i}^{(2)},

where the maximum is achieved by using an arbitrary maximally entangled
state
\rho
at the input.

Appendix B
Proofs

Lemma B.1
.

Let
\bar{B}
and
E
be isomorphic systems each corresponding to
n
qubits. The quantum channel
\mathcal{M}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{E}
acts as a projector on the space of Hermitian matrices over
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{B}E}
. Let
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
denote its image and kernel, respectively. Every state
\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\bar{B}E)
can thus be decomposed as
\rho=\rho_{0}+\rho_{1}
with
\rho_{0}\in E_{0},\rho_{1}\in E_{1}
. Then:

\displaystyle\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}N\in E_{0}:\\
\operatorname{Tr}(N)=0\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\bar{B}E)}\left\|\rho_{1}+N\right\|_{1}=

\displaystyle\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}N\in E_{0}:\\
\operatorname{Tr}(N)=0\end{subarray}}\ \max_{\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\bar{B}E)}\left\|\rho_{1}+N\right\|_{1}=\max_{\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\bar{B}E)}\left\|\rho_{1}\right\|_{1}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\otimes n}(X)=\sum_{b\in\{0,1\}^{n}}P_{b}^{(n)}XP_{b}^{(n)},

where
P_{b}^{(n)}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}{P_{b_{i}}}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{M}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{E}
acts on Hermitian matrices over
\mathcal{H}_{\bar{B}E}
by selecting diagonal blocks.
E_{0}
is therefore to the space of block-diagonal matrices with respect to the canonical basis
b\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, and conversely
E_{1}
is the space of matrices whose non-zero blocks are off diagonal. The goal is now to prove that the infimum is attained for
N=0
. We must first ensure that the infimum is a minimum. We have
\left\|\rho_{1}\right\|_{1}\leq\left\|\rho\right\|_{1}+\left\|\rho_{0}\right\|_{1}=2
, so when
N=0
we have
\left\|\rho_{1}+N\right\|_{1}\leq 2
whereas when
\|N\|_{1}>4
we have
\left\|\rho_{1}+N\right\|_{1}\geq|\|\rho_{1}\|_{1}-\|N\|_{1}|>2
. Therefore it suffices to consider
\|N\|_{1}\leq 4
. Since the subset of elements of
E_{0}
of trace 0 and norm no greater than 4 is a compact space, and
N\mapsto\max_{\rho\in\mathsf{St}(\bar{B}E)}\left\|\rho_{1}+N\right\|_{1}
is a continuous function, the infimum is a minimum. Moreover, it can be shown that the set of all
N
for which the minimum is attained is a convex set.
Choose
N\in E_{0}
that reaches the infimum. By definition of
E_{0}
,
N
has the following block diagonal form:

N=\begin{bmatrix}N_{1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&N_{2^{n}}\end{bmatrix}.

The trace norm is stable under application of unitary channels, and
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
are stable under application of block diagonal unitary channels. Therefore, we may assume without loss of generality that every
N_{i}
is a diagonal matrix:

N=\begin{bmatrix}D_{1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&D_{2^{n}}\end{bmatrix}.

Let

D_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&\lambda_{2^{n}}\end{bmatrix},

be the first diagonal block. By applying the unitary channel that permutes the first block according to
\pi\in S(2^{n})
, the following matrix is also optimal:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}D_{1}^{\pi}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&D_{2^{n}}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\pi\in S(2^{n}),\qquad D_{1}^{\pi}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{\pi(1)}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&\lambda_{\pi(2^{n})}\end{bmatrix}.

Therefore, using the fact that the set of optimal channels is convex, the following convex combination is also an optimal matrix:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2^{n})!}\sum_{\pi\in S(2^{n})}\begin{bmatrix}D_{1}^{\pi}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&D_{2^{n}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{1}I&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&D_{2^{n}}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}\lambda_{i}.

By repeating the same process for each block,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{1}I&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&\beta_{2^{n}}I\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&\beta_{2^{n}}\end{bmatrix}\otimes I

is also optimal. Moreover, since the infimum is taken over matrices of trace 0, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}\beta_{i}=0
. We now proceed similarly, but permute the blocks themselves rather than the terms within one block. For
\pi\in S(2^{n})
, let
U_{\pi}
be the corresponding permutation matrix. We consider the quantum channel
\mathcal{C}_{\pi}\coloneqq U_{\pi}(\cdot)U_{\pi}^{{\dagger}}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{E}
, which preserves the spaces
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
. Since the trace norm is stable under application of unitary channels,

\mathcal{C}_{\pi}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{1}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&\beta_{2^{n}}\end{bmatrix}\otimes I\right)=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{\pi(1)}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&\beta_{\pi(2^{n})}\end{bmatrix}\otimes I

is also optimal. By convexity of the set of optimal channels,

\frac{1}{(2^{n})!}\sum_{\pi\in S(2^{n})}\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{\pi(1)}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&\beta_{\pi(2^{n})}\end{bmatrix}\otimes I=0,

is also optimal, which concludes the proof.
∎
